- [x] **App-authored haptics have no mute preference.** The main target's shared helper always instantiates and plays its feedback generator (`ios/Wisconsin/Core/Haptics.swift:4-33`), and the kiosk target duplicates the same unconditional behavior (`ios/Wisconsin/KioskOnly/KioskOnlyApp.swift:168-190`). Settings offers theme, privacy, support, notification, and system-permission destinations but no haptics control (`ios/Wisconsin/Views/SettingsView.swift:12-136`). With 215 explicit calls across ordinary navigation, filtering, editing, mutations, scanning, and kiosk work, relying only on device-level behavior doesn't meet Apple's recommendation to let people mute app haptics. Add one device-local Haptics preference, enforce it inside both helpers and SwiftUI sensory-feedback call sites, and leave every visible/spoken outcome intact when muted.
- [x] **The Admin role-preview action uses a disclosure chevron even though it changes mode immediately.** “Preview as Student” is a plain Button that starts the signed read-only session as soon as it is selected, but its manually drawn `chevron.forward` advertises navigation into another settings page (`ios/Wisconsin/Views/SettingsView.swift:62-77`). Remove the disclosure accessory or replace it with an action treatment that truthfully predicts the immediate app-wide transition; the active preview's Exit action already avoids the false accessory.
- [x] **General Settings has no standard hardware-keyboard entry route.** The only source route is Home's Profile avatar and sheet, followed by Profile's gear button (`ios/Wisconsin/Views/HomeView.swift:346-361`, `:493-496`; `ios/Wisconsin/Views/ProfileView.swift:64-70`); the main target declares no Settings keyboard command or `UIKeyCommand`. Apple notes that people commonly expect Command-Comma with a physical keyboard. Preserve the visible route, but add the standard shortcut so iPad keyboard users don't have to leave their current tab and traverse the Home-only modal path.
- [x] **The Mac companion unconditionally inserts its only native entry point in the menu bar.** The app declares `MenuBarExtra` without an `isInserted` binding and `LSUIElement = true`, so it always inserts the extra while running and supplies no Dock or app-switcher recovery route (`macos/GearOps/GearOpsApp.swift:12-26`; `macos/GearOps/Supporting/Info.plist:25-28`). General Settings lets people hide only the booking count, not the extra itself (`macos/GearOps/SettingsView.swift:101-110`; `macos/GearOps/AppPreferences.swift:5-36`), even though Apple asks apps to let people decide whether to keep an extra and to avoid depending on its presence because macOS can hide extras when space is constrained. The web control room preserves the underlying operational tasks, so this is nonblocking; add a recoverable user-controlled insertion setting or another discoverable native way to reopen Settings and restore the extra without weakening the accepted menu-only utility boundary.
- [x] **The shared `tap` pattern repurposes a collision haptic as generic command feedback.** Both target helpers define `tap()` as `UIImpactFeedbackGenerator(style: .light)` and describe/use it for ordinary primary presses (`ios/Wisconsin/Core/Haptics.swift:30-33`, `ios/Wisconsin/KioskOnly/KioskOnlyApp.swift:187-190`). Current call sites include retrying push registration, copying a license or asset code, applying an extension preset, opening Search, toggling scanner affordances, and tapping activation digits (`ios/Wisconsin/Views/NotificationSettingsView.swift:201-212`, `ios/Wisconsin/Views/LicensesView.swift:613-622`, `ios/Wisconsin/Views/ExtendBookingSheet.swift:124-131`, `ios/Wisconsin/Views/HomeView.swift:1329-1341`, `ios/Wisconsin/Kiosk/KioskActivationView.swift:228-237`). Apple defines impact feedback as a physical collision metaphor, not a generic click. Remove gratuitous command pulses; use selection only for an actual value change and success/warning/error only when an outcome warrants it.
- [x] **Some native selection controls receive an extra or delayed app-authored selection pulse.** Schedule attaches `.sensoryFeedback(.selection)` to its standard segmented Picker (`ios/Wisconsin/Views/ScheduleView.swift:1301-1325`), while Trade Board manually calls `Haptics.selection()` inside a native Toggle setter (`ios/Wisconsin/Views/Schedule/TradeBoardSheet.swift:357-369`). Every notification Toggle also causes `NotificationPrefsViewModel.save` to emit a selection haptic only after the network write returns (`ios/Wisconsin/Views/NotificationSettingsView.swift:55-79`, `:257-289`; `ios/Wisconsin/Core/Preferences.swift:151-166`). Apple states that standard toggles and pickers already play their designed haptics; the additional calls can double the immediate pulse or detach it from the causal value change. Let the control own selection feedback and reserve the later generator for a distinct visible success or failure outcome.

## Clean component evidence

- **Tab shell:** five compact navigation-only destinations, Search uses the platform search role, labels use SF Symbols, and urgency badges include accessibility labels.
- **Sidebar:** `.sidebarAdaptable` provides a regular-width two-level Team/Resources hierarchy while compact width returns to the tab bar; secondary destinations remain reachable through Browse.
- **Search:** native `.searchable`, direct focus, debouncing, categorized results, clear/cancel behavior, and partial/stale failure handling are present.
- **Menus and dialogs:** destructive actions are generally separated, role-marked, named, and confirmed; unsaved work offers an explicit keep-editing escape.
- **Sheets:** camera work uses full-screen presentation; resizable sheets expose grabbers; drafts and in-flight mutations guard dismissal.
- **Lists and tables:** inspected rows use native list structure, text-led hierarchy, consistent navigation/selection affordances, recoverable empty states, and combined accessibility labels.
- **Collections:** the kiosk roster solves column count from available width, preserves a 46-point minimum, scrolls instead of shrinking further, alphabetizes names, disambiguates duplicate first names, and makes each tile one labeled Button. Badge and filter grids use adaptive columns, full-tile controls, section counts, selected traits, empty-state recovery, and combined accessibility labels. Static metric and unit grids do not imply interaction.
- **Pickers:** short mutually exclusive choices use segmented controls; longer categorical choices use labeled menu or form pickers; date-only choices use native DatePicker controls with valid lower bounds where required; photo selection uses PhotosPicker; selected values remain visible and server-owned option lists remain authoritative.
- **Segmented controls:** every inspected control switches one closely related value or view, uses two to four short text labels, preserves one selected segment, and updates content immediately. The reservation pickup-location control is bounded to the two accepted primary locations rather than an unbounded server list. Controls carry visible context or an accessibility label where the visible title is intentionally omitted.
- **Toggles:** notification switches update optimistically and revert on failure; Schedule and Trade filters take effect immediately; availability and call-window switches reveal or remove their dependent fields; visible labels state the on/off condition without relying on color, and intentionally hidden labels receive explicit accessibility labels.
- **Text fields:** email, username, password, phone, one-time-code, and scanner fields use appropriate content types, keyboards, capitalization, and correction behavior; multi-step forms provide submit labels and focus routing; password fields preserve secure entry and explicit visibility controls; bounded numeric identity fields filter and count input; validation and retry messages remain adjacent to the affected form.
- **Text views:** the three multiline-entry surfaces are short operational notes rather than document editors; each uses a vertically growing native TextField with a bounded two-to-eight-line range, visible context, a concise prompt, and preserved input during submission failure. The controls remain inside scrollable forms or sheets, and posting disables the affected note field while work is in flight.
- **Virtual keyboards:** email, phone, numeric identity, password, search, and scanner recovery fields request the expected keyboard; action keys use Continue, Next, Search, Go, or Done according to the task; long forms dismiss interactively; the Welcome flow adds an explicit keyboard Done control for number-pad fields; and kiosk activation deliberately uses its visible custom number pad over a hidden one-time-code field. The Text fields registration-focus finding also blocks this pass and is counted once as the same repair item.
- **Labels:** operational controls generally pair concise verb-led text with an SF Symbol that reinforces the same action; state labels include text instead of relying on color; dynamic failure and status labels remain readable; icon-only controls supply explicit accessibility labels; combined rows replace fragmented child announcements with task-oriented spoken summaries.
- **Progress indicators:** determinate progress is used for measurable badge, scan, pickup, and return completion; indeterminate indicators are reserved for work with unknown duration; mutation buttons retain task text while showing activity; controls disable duplicate submission; launch, passkey, guide, roster, and ranking waits include adjacent context; failure states replace progress with bounded retry paths.
- **Badging:** Schedule and Gear tab badges carry task-relevant numeric counts, disappear at zero, reset at session boundaries, refresh through lifecycle and user-driven reads, and include explicit spoken counts. Home notification and Schedule trade indicators use a dot or filled-symbol state with exact accessibility counts. The app does not set a separate app-icon badge, avoiding a second potentially stale count. Achievement medallions remain product content covered by the Collections pass.
- **Page controls:** no native or custom page control, page-styled TabView, or paging scroll target exists in the iOS sources. The apparent candidates are correctly modeled as free-scrolling filters, badge shelves, tables, and code blocks, or as sequential Reservation and Welcome tasks with labeled progress and explicit Back/Continue controls. None represents a flat list of peer pages that needs a bottom-centered position indicator.
- **Scroll views:** inspected surfaces retain system scrolling gestures and avoid same-axis nesting. Horizontal filters, badge shelves, guide tables, and code blocks are nested only inside vertical readers; clipped neighboring content communicates overflow. Compact kiosk layouts introduce scrolling only when content no longer fits, long operational lists expose visible indicators, and scan flows automatically reveal only the newly added item. Form-heavy screens use normal scroll containers and interactive keyboard dismissal without custom gesture interception; no page-control/scroll-indicator conflict exists.
- **Sliders:** profile-photo cropping is the only slider surface. It uses native bounded controls for zoom and two-axis positioning, keeps minimum values on the leading side, updates the preview continuously, disables an axis when movement is unavailable, clamps the image inside the crop, and provides slider alternatives to direct drag and pinch gestures. Each slider has a concise task label; the directional spoken-value refinement is recorded as P2.
- **Steppers:** counted reservation items use explicit minus/value/plus groups beside the affected item, update in increments of one, disable at zero and the available maximum, expose item-specific increment/decrement labels, provide immediate numeric and haptic feedback, and clamp again in the view model to `0...availableQuantity`. The existing Buttons finding for 28–36-point reservation quantity controls also blocks this pass and is counted once as the same repair item.
- **Refresh content controls:** list, detail, report, guide, Schedule, and diagnostic surfaces use SwiftUI’s native pull-to-refresh interaction and await their asynchronous handlers so the system indicator tracks real work. Automatic appearance, foreground, reconnection, freshness-window, and kiosk timer updates keep people from owning all freshness; stale content remains visible when refresh fails, with bounded Retry affordances. Hardened resource and paginated lists give explicit refreshes newest-request ownership. The remaining dropped-pull inconsistency is recorded as P2.
- **Gauges:** no native or custom status gauge exists in the iOS sources. Utilization appears as a labeled percentage and analytical charts rather than a misleading capacity control; status distribution and custody-day rankings are charts with explicit labels and row values. Badge, Welcome, pickup, return, and scan visuals represent task or goal completion and correctly remain determinate progress indicators with spoken current/total values instead of gauges.
- **Color wells:** no `ColorPicker`, `UIColorWell`, `UIColorPickerViewController`, or custom swatch-selection control exists in the native sources. The app does not offer color authoring, so omitting a color well is appropriate. Brand, status, venue, staffing, chart, and badge colors are display-only semantic roles; inspected surfaces pair them with text, symbols, rail position, or explicit accessibility labels rather than implying they are editable or relying on color alone.
- **Disclosure controls:** item Details and Attachments plus Trade Board Waiting/Blocked and Resolved sections use native `DisclosureGroup` controls with labels that name the content they reveal. The Scoreboard breakdown uses a full-width 44-point custom disclosure button whose text changes between “Show all” and “Show fewer,” and Overdue leaderboard rows pair their changing chevrons with explicit expand/collapse accessibility hints. Navigation chevrons and reservation minimize/cart controls perform navigation or presentation rather than disclosure and remain labeled for those tasks.
- **Context menus:** all inspected surfaces use native SwiftUI menus, familiar symbols, and context-specific availability gates. Notifications, Items reservation/favorite actions, booking actions, Schedule follow state, and event sharing have equivalent tap, swipe, detail, or overflow routes. Booking cancellation and crew removal/deletion actions appear last and use destructive roles. Event cards and crew rows match their preview content shapes to rounded-card and rectangular-row geometry, and no inspected item combines a custom context menu with an edit menu.
- **Edit menus:** editable SwiftUI text and secure fields retain the system-provided edit menu; the app does not install a global `UIEditMenuInteraction`, `UIMenuController`, responder-action filter, or custom replacement menu. Guides, crew notes, account identity, welcome email, badge names, license codes, and diagnostic values selectively enable native text selection for useful noneditable content. Guide code blocks and the active-license card preserve system selection while adding a bounded whole-value copy shortcut, kiosk activation provides a visible paste route for its intentionally hidden one-time-code field, and no editable control also carries the app's custom context menus.
- **Home Screen quick actions:** the dynamic menu stays within Apple's four-item limit and exposes high-value Scan, My Gear, Schedule, and New Reservation destinations. Titles are succinct, subtitles either explain the destination or predictably surface overdue, due-today, and next-shift context from the same account-scoped widget snapshot, and each item uses a familiar monochrome SF Symbol. Collaborator capabilities filter unavailable destinations, sign-out clears the menu, subtitle refresh cannot reintroduce a filtered action, and the shared App Intent handoff provides capability-gated destination routing once an invocation reaches it. The unsupported scene-lifecycle entry point remains the sole source blocker.
- **Activity views:** Event Detail is the only activity-view surface and uses SwiftUI's system `ShareLink`, which automatically supplies the platform sheet/popover behavior and cancellation semantics. The Share Event command uses the familiar share symbol and a succinct label; its plain-text payload includes only the event's display title, localized date/time or all-day state, and venue, omitting crew identities, internal notes, working-copy state, and other account data. The adjacent Copy Event Details command uses the same bounded payload, and both the overflow and event-card context menu stay consistent. The remaining visible-entry-point gap is recorded as P2.
- **Pull-down buttons:** all inspected controls use native SwiftUI `Menu` presentation, concise purpose labels, familiar symbols, and role- or data-based availability. Primary actions such as Post Trade and Favorite remain visible rather than moving into overflow; destructive Delete and Revert commands retain destructive roles and confirmation; related selectors in Guides, Trade Board, Users, and Bookings use Picker or Toggle semantics inside their filter menus. Event and item overflow contents remain directly related to their trigger, while the two refinements above address selection semantics and undersized conditional menus.
- **Pop-up buttons:** Settings theme, Scoreboard facets, Schedule sport, Guides focus/sort, Trade Board area, Users role, and Bookings status/sort selectors use native Picker selection, useful default values, flat mutually exclusive option sets, and labels or surrounding headings that predict their effect. Form and card placements let the platform display the current selection, while compact toolbar filter menus provide active-state tint plus explicit spoken values where the visual label stays purpose-oriented. The existing 96-value time-selector P1 blocks this pass, and the Items command-menu P2 from the Pull-down Buttons pass is the same missing pop-up-button repair rather than a new finding.
- **Radio buttons:** Apple places radio-button guidance under macOS rather than iOS/iPadOS, and the native targets contain no radio control, custom filled-dot radio group, AppKit radio API, or radio accessibility role. Short mutually exclusive choices use segmented controls; longer choices use pop-up buttons or checkmarked list rows. Circular checkmark candidates are correctly differentiated: reservation events are multi-select rows (`ios/Wisconsin/Views/CreateBooking/CreateBookingEventViews.swift:215-269`), while Trade Board posting makes the entire shift row a single-choice Button and exposes `.isSelected` (`ios/Wisconsin/Views/Schedule/PostTradeSheet.swift:151-177`, `:307-337`). Password and kiosk circles communicate requirement/completion state rather than selection.
- **Action sheets:** all 28 inspected surfaces use SwiftUI `confirmationDialog`, avoiding deprecated `ActionSheet`, `UIActionSheet`, and hand-configured UIKit popover anchoring. Draft exits offer Save, Discard, and Keep Editing; destructive booking, account, availability, item, passkey, schedule, license, calendar-link, and kiosk actions use destructive roles with an explicit safe cancel; constructive claims and assignments remain default actions. Choices are bounded and do not require scrolling, dialogs are triggered by deliberate actions, mutation work is kept out of the presentation body, and SwiftUI supplies the platform-specific bottom sheet or anchored iPad popover. The two refinements above cover the lone unexpected-error misuse and titles that may grow beyond one line.
- **Popovers:** the native targets contain no general-purpose SwiftUI `.popover`, custom popover controller, editable popover, warning popover, nested popover, or popover that launches another modal. The seven popover presentations are short TipKit discovery messages attached directly to New Reservation, Minimize, Scan, Resume, Trade Board, Shift Calendar, and My Availability controls. Each uses `MaxDisplayCount(1)`, invalidates when its feature is used, and keeps content to a concise title and one explanatory sentence; later tips require relevant usage-event donations. Preferred arrow edges place top-toolbar tips below their source and the bottom draft-card tip above its source, while conditional call sites prevent duplicate New Reservation attachments from coexisting. TipKit owns transient dismissal and compact presentation, and performance fixtures suppress tips for deterministic captures.
- **Navigation bars:** the app consistently uses SwiftUI `NavigationStack`, native destination links, system back behavior, concise titles, and platform toolbar placements; no pushed destination hides the system Back button or replaces `UINavigationBar`. Large titles remain on broad browsing roots such as Items, Users, and Browse, while dense operational and modal screens use inline titles to preserve action space. Modal flows identify each step and keep primary work in the content area. The empty pushed titles and reservation wizard's text Back control remain the two source blockers; the latter is the same repair item already recorded by the Toolbars pass.
- **Split views:** the signed-in app delegates its only split-like navigation surface to SwiftUI's `.sidebarAdaptable` tab style at regular width and returns to `.tabBarOnly` in compact environments, matching Apple's preference to reserve adjacent panes for available horizontal space. The value-bound TabView keeps the current regular-width destination highlighted, Team and Resources groups stay succinct, system tab customization persists in AppStorage, and every sidebar-only destination also has a capability-appropriate route through compact Browse. There is no bespoke pane divider, fixed column width, hidden sidebar toggle, or unrelated two-column layout presented as hierarchical navigation. The explicit compact-width reset above is the sole source blocker; iPad resizing and customization still need runtime proof.
- **Path controls:** Apple marks path controls as unsupported on iOS and iPadOS, and the native targets correctly contain no `NSPathControl`, path cells, breadcrumb strip, chevron-separated file location, or custom file-system-path control. SwiftUI `NavigationPath` values in Browse, Items, Guides, Users, Schedule, Home, Search, and scanner diagnostics are nonvisual stack state paired with `NavigationStack` and `navigationDestination`; they are not path-control UI. The inspected chevrons are individual row-navigation, disclosure, or calendar-direction affordances, and the app has no native file-hierarchy workflow that requires a macOS-style path bar. Web breadcrumbs remain outside this native component scope.
- **Windows:** both native products use a system SwiftUI `WindowGroup` with no custom frame or window controls. The main app remains eligible for normal iPad full-screen and windowed presentation, declares every iPad orientation, and uses adaptive tab/sidebar and scrolling structures rather than fixed screen bounds. Its single-window model is appropriate for the shared account workspace; no independent document or auxiliary task currently justifies opening extra windows by default. Scene lifecycle work refreshes authoritative data, autosaves an active reservation draft on backgrounding, and installs a window-sized privacy shield that follows the installed scene. The kiosk is intentionally a separate iPad-only counter appliance, sets a practical future window minimum, and adapts scan rails plus the operator hub below its compact breakpoint. The existing sidebar-context-loss P1 also blocks this pass because normal main-app window resizing can redirect the user to Home; it is the same repair item counted by Split Views. The kiosk full-screen deprecation is the nonblocking migration risk above, while actual iPad window controls, common resized widths, and physical mounted behavior remain runtime gates.
- **Multitasking:** the main target participates in system iPad multitasking, declares every iPad orientation, and does not request full-screen-only compatibility. Its SwiftUI scene autosaves an active reservation draft before suspension, refreshes authoritative session and account state on return, stops scanner torch activity while inactive, cancels long-lived polling cooperatively, and covers inactive app-switcher snapshots with a scene-bound privacy shield. The adaptive tab/sidebar and scrolling structures support windowed presentation, and the separate kiosk target deliberately retains its accepted mounted landscape appliance contract while keeping compact window layouts in source. The existing sidebar-context-loss P1 also blocks this pass because ordinary resizing can redirect the person to Home. The kiosk migration deadline and missing scene-route restoration are P2; common half-, third-, quadrant-, rotation-, interruption-, and eviction/relaunch configurations remain runtime gates.
- **Layout:** the main target uses SwiftUI system containers, semantic spacing, safe-area-aware bars and insets, every iPad orientation, and a convertible `.sidebarAdaptable` tab shell. Backgrounds, camera previews, and modal scrims are the only inspected content that deliberately extends beyond safe areas; ordinary controls remain inside them, and the main app keeps the status bar visible. Representative booking, item, profile, settings, Welcome, Schedule, and kiosk scan layouts either stack at accessibility sizes, use `ViewThatFits`, allow vertical growth, scale fixed gutters, or scroll when content no longer fits. The existing sidebar-context-loss and kiosk identity-grid findings are the two blockers: one changes the logical destination during an ordinary size-class transition, and the other bypasses the accessibility-size roster layout on a required identity screen. The kiosk full-screen deadline is the shared P2. Half-, third-, quadrant-, rotation-, smallest/largest Dynamic Type, Display Zoom, long-content, and left-to-right/right-to-left runtime configurations remain open.
- **Typography:** body copy, metadata, controls, and long operational prose predominantly use the system text-style ramp, regular-to-bold weights, semantic foreground styles, and SF Symbols that inherit adjacent weight and scale. Gotham Black and Bold are limited to brand, identity, and high-level content emphasis; both bundled targets register the same two font files, and their shared helpers use `Font.custom(_:size:relativeTo:)` with a scalable system-font fallback. The kiosk centralizes its hierarchy into a named type ramp, keeps Gotham out of small prose, and reserves tracked uppercase text for short overlines, state labels, and chips. Representative booking, item, profile, settings, Welcome, Schedule, and kiosk layouts stack or relax truncation at accessibility sizes. The existing kiosk identity-grid P1 also blocks this pass because its scaled name text remains in a dense multi-column, scale-down composition instead of the idle roster's one-column fallback. Small fixed-size avatar initials are the P2 above; largest accessibility sizes, Bold Text, and real viewing-distance legibility remain runtime gates.
- **Writing:** primary controls are concise and verb-led; multi-step flows use stable Back, Continue, Save, Create, and Done language; destructive confirmations name the affected reservation, shift, account, item, or availability change; and most errors state what failed beside a Retry or alternative path without blaming the person. Empty states distinguish an empty collection from filtered no-results and usually identify the next useful action. Operational vocabulary stays aligned with the product contract: reservation describes planned pickup, checkout describes custody, return describes ending custody, Schedule and Trade Board retain their domain names, and student-facing surfaces avoid schema enums. The six P2 refinements cover two menu labels, reservation failure presentation/action language, the kiosk camera's Done/Close mismatch, first-person or technical recovery language, the “Checkout Gear” noun/verb collision, and inconsistent recovery labels. Runtime review remains open for truncation, VoiceOver phrasing, permission-alert handoff, and comprehension at the kiosk's physical viewing distance.
- **Color:** the main target follows the system appearance by default and offers explicit System, Light, and Dark choices. System semantic backgrounds, labels, separators, and materials carry most hierarchy; brand, status, marker, and chart roles use centralized dynamic providers with separate light/dark values. Status badges and rails are accompanied by text, symbols, dates, counts, or row identity, chart segments retain numeric labels and combined accessibility descriptions, selected controls use native selected traits or checkmarks, and the kiosk deliberately forces a named fixed-dark surface/text scale while preserving labeled semantic status roles. The custom-fill foreground-contrast issue above now blocks this pass after numeric verification; the reversible Return License action's destructive red treatment and the month calendar's hue-only venue dots remain its two P2 refinements. Light/dark screenshots, Increase Contrast, Differentiate Without Color, grayscale/color-filter, and physical kiosk viewing remain runtime gates.
- **Dark Mode:** the signed-in app respects the system appearance by default and applies its saved System, Light, or Dark choice at the root scene. Standard backgrounds, grouped surfaces, labels, separators, controls, materials, and SF Symbols use semantic APIs; custom brand, status, marker, and chart colors provide distinct light/dark values. Launch, sign-in, password setup, camera, and the separate counter kiosk intentionally remain dark, and their fixed colors are confined to dark branded, image, or camera contexts; launch assets match the branded splash rather than disappearing against an adaptive surface. Catalog product photos retain white frames by design, while placeholders remain semantic and dark-adaptive. The explicit-white/custom-fill contrast blocker above prevents source readiness; appearance switching while the app is active, elevated modal surfaces, Reduce Transparency, Increase Contrast, and representative asset legibility remain runtime gates.
- **Materials:** system TabView, navigation, menus, sheets, popovers, alerts, and toolbars own their platform Liquid Glass automatically, while custom `.glass` and `.glassProminent` styles remain attached to actual buttons and choice controls. Content-layer depth uses semantic grouped backgrounds, standard `regular`/`thin`/`ultraThin` materials, or `.bar` for safe-area action bars: the login card, toasts, informational banners, badge celebration, camera controls, photo viewer, booking extension bar, and reservation/event action bars all retain system adaptation rather than hard-coded blur effects. Foregrounds over materials predominantly use hierarchical primary/secondary styles, and animated depth effects honor Reduce Motion. The kiosk deliberately uses a fixed-dark named surface/stroke scale for always-on performance, then uses glass for its controls; only the two informational containers above blur that boundary. Reduce Transparency, Increase Contrast, live-camera brightness, modal elevation, and iOS/iPadOS 26 Liquid Glass appearance remain runtime gates.
- **Motion:** system navigation, sheets, menus, controls, progress indicators, and Liquid Glass own familiar platform movement. Custom motion is short and tied to state or feedback: filters settle quickly, count changes use numeric transitions, loading placeholders pulse or shimmer only while data is pending, success and badge moments are dismissible, and kiosk auto-return exposes both countdown text and a Done shortcut. Skeleton pulse/shimmer, launch accents, pre-prompt symbol bounce, badge entrances, kiosk screen transitions, sleep-mode pixel shift, scan-stage springs, automatic list scrolling, countdown bars, and press scaling all stop, become static, or reduce to a fade when Reduce Motion is on. Haptics, visible text, symbols, accessibility announcements, and stable final states keep animation from carrying meaning alone. The unguarded common-task banner translations above block this pass; the optional photo-viewer spring zoom is P2. Simulator Reduce Motion, repeated rapid scans, interrupted transitions, VoiceOver announcements, and physical kiosk viewing remain runtime gates.
- **Icons:** the app predominantly uses SF Symbols through native Tab, Label, Menu, toolbar, content-unavailable, and button APIs, keeping familiar metaphors aligned with adjacent text and system font weight. Top-level tabs have stable names and recognizable symbols, icon-only controls generally add explicit accessibility labels, directional chevrons and arrows remain system symbols that can adapt to reading direction, and the only inspected named raster artwork is the Badgers brand mark rather than a competing interface-icon set. Badge artwork is centralized in one Lucide-to-SF-Symbol map, remains one-to-one across the shelf and celebration, and has catalog-input coverage. The five nonexistent symbol names above block this pass because their six states render no glyph; the kiosk camera's existing Done/xmark/cancel mismatch is the one P2 semantic refinement. Representative rendering modes, selected states, VoiceOver labels, right-to-left mirroring, and physical kiosk viewing remain runtime gates.
- **Images:** bundled Badgers and launch artwork ships proportional 1x/2x/3x PNG variants, while the app icon retains its required 1024-pixel source. Remote list imagery is usually downsampled to its actual display scale, cached within explicit memory/disk limits, cancelled with view lifetime, evicted under memory pressure, and cleared across account boundaries. Catalog product photography uses fit rather than fill so equipment is not cropped; avatars use centered circular fill with initials fallbacks; guide images preserve aspect ratio, decode to the measured column width, display author-provided alt text, and expose loading and unavailable states. Interactive item heroes are wrapped in labeled Buttons before opening the bounded full-screen zoom viewer, and profile selection uses the system photo picker followed by explicit crop controls that normalize orientation and redraw the output, stripping source metadata. The device-scaled profile export blocks this pass because its output contradicts the intended pixel budget and can exceed the upload contract. Blank failed thumbnails and missing bundled color profiles are the two P2 refinements. Actual asset color, crop safety, remote failure, slow-network, memory-pressure, 2x/3x sharpness, and physical-device rendering remain runtime gates.
- **Image views:** static artwork uses SwiftUI Image for display only, while the one tap-enabled item hero is correctly wrapped in a system Button with an explicit label and hint before it opens the viewer. Product shots use aspect fit to keep the whole item visible; avatars and small identity thumbnails use centered aspect fill inside consistent circles or rounded rectangles; placeholders match each content type; and no inspected text is composited directly over variable photography. Guide image views measure their actual column, downsample before drawing, preserve aspect ratio, surface authored alternative text, and distinguish loading from unavailable content. The profile crop's drag and magnification gestures manipulate the image itself rather than disguising an instantaneous command as a tappable picture. No P0/P1 image-view blocker remains. Blank failed discovery thumbnails and the full-screen viewer's missing content/loading/failure semantics are the two P2 refinements. Actual zoom gestures, slow/failing loads, VoiceOver modal context, rotation, text contrast near imagery, and physical-device sharpness remain runtime gates.
- **Image wells:** Apple marks image wells as unsupported on iOS and iPadOS, and neither native target imports AppKit image-well APIs, creates an editable image container, accepts an image through drag and drop, or reads/writes image data through the clipboard. The image-related pasteboard calls are bounded text-copy or kiosk activation-code routes, not image editing. Profile photography correctly uses the system `PhotosPicker`, opens a dedicated crop sheet, and requires explicit Save or Cancel rather than making the displayed avatar itself behave like a desktop image well. No image well is required by the current mobile or counter workflow, so omission is correct and no P0/P1/P2 finding applies.
- **Image buttons:** instantaneous image and symbol actions use native SwiftUI `Button`, `Menu`, `ShareLink`, or toolbar items instead of attaching command behavior directly to an image. Familiar SF Symbols communicate close, add, remove, share, scan, and navigation actions; inspected icon-only controls provide explicit accessibility labels; and custom kiosk press styling supplies visible scale and opacity feedback. Direct gestures on photography are confined to crop and zoom manipulation rather than app commands. The existing 44-point interaction-target repair item blocks this pass: reservation dismiss, stepper, and recommendation buttons are 28–36 points, and the photo viewer's image-only close control is 38 points. No additional image-button finding was created. Runtime proof remains open for hit regions, press states, VoiceOver and Voice Control labels, pointer interaction, and right-to-left behavior.
- **Right to left:** the current product scope is explicitly English-only, and its source largely stays safe for future mirroring by using system SwiftUI navigation, controls, sheets, alerts, lists, logical leading/trailing alignment and padding, locale-aware date/currency formatting, and SF Symbols that supply RTL variants. No view forces a left-to-right layout, manually flips photographs or brand artwork, or mirrors the Badgers logo; progress indicators, sliders, toolbar placements, and disclosure/navigation controls remain system-owned. The one P2 above is confined to Schedule's custom month calendar, whose fixed English weekday arrays, Sunday-first offset, and physical swipe mapping need localization-aware replacement before Arabic or Hebrew can be claimed. Forced RTL, mixed-script paragraphs, localized numerals, mirrored ordered controls, and representative navigation/icon rendering remain runtime gates.
- **SF Symbols:** the native source contains 515 system-symbol call sites across `Tab`, `Label`, `Image`, menus, toolbars, content-unavailable states, App Intents, and kiosk controls. The iOS 26 deployment baseline avoids back-deployment feature gaps; tab and toolbar containers choose their preferred variants; most symbols inherit adjacent system-font weight and automatic rendering; the one explicit multicolor bell uses a system symbol; and the three symbol animations are brief, purposeful, and gated by Reduce Motion. No custom symbol set exists, and the raster Badgers/Launch artwork plus AppIcon remain separate from SF Symbols, avoiding prohibited logo or trademark use. The centralized badge map is one-to-one across its two consumers and has catalog-input coverage, but that coverage does not validate the resulting SF Symbol names. The five nonexistent names above therefore block this pass, while the kiosk camera's Done/xmark mismatch is the shared P2. Rendering modes, fill/selection variants, Dark Mode, Increase Contrast, RTL/script variants, VoiceOver labels, animations, and physical-device legibility remain runtime gates.
- **Spatial layout:** Apple defines this foundation specifically for the infinite canvas of Apple Vision Pro and marks it unsupported on iOS and iPadOS. Wisconsin Creative declares only iOS application, test, and extension targets: the main product targets iPhone and iPad, while the counter kiosk targets iPad only. Both app entry points use a conventional SwiftUI `WindowGroup`; the native source contains no visionOS target, `ImmersiveSpace`, volume, RealityKit, 3D-model, spatial-event, or spatial-input implementation. The kiosk shell's comment about “spatial continuity” describes stable two-dimensional layout geometry, not a visionOS feature. Camera scanners likewise remain flat iOS capture interfaces rather than immersive experiences. No current mobile or counter workflow requires a visionOS surface, so omitting spatial layout is correct and no P0/P1/P2 finding applies.
- **Accessibility:** the native source has a substantial accessibility baseline: 751 explicit accessibility modifiers, system SwiftUI controls and text styles, 31 files that read Reduce Motion, visual text paired with operational haptics, spoken kiosk scan and success feedback, labeled icon-only controls, and no audio/video-only content path. Accepted decision D-053 keeps accessibility Dynamic Type sizes supported-but-not-optimized for this bounded internal audience; this pass does not re-raise the accepted Schedule venue-line squeeze, and still treats loss of function, VoiceOver, contrast, target size, and motion as ship-bar concerns. Nine source blockers remain: missing VoiceOver destination identity; two distinct undersized-target repairs covering frequent controls and the month calendar; the kiosk identity grid's missing accessibility-size fallback; registration focus entering a disabled field; crew lifecycle commands available only by long press; insufficient custom-fill contrast; unguarded feedback motion; and the newly recorded unannounced transient toast. Six nonblocking refinements remain shared with earlier passes: manual Items selection semantics, color-only calendar venue dots, unlabeled initial loaders, generic crop-slider values, the full-screen photo viewer's missing content/recovery semantics, and sub-11-point avatar initials. VoiceOver, Voice Control, Switch Control, Full Keyboard Access, Dynamic Type usability, Increase Contrast, Differentiate Without Color, Reduce Motion, Accessibility Inspector, and physical-device assistive-technology proof remain runtime gates.
- **App icons:** the main iPhone/iPad app compiles the single approved Icon Composer package: an opaque Display-P3 red gradient background and one crisp vector Block W foreground with clearly defined edges, system-owned glass/specular treatment, and no baked corner mask, photograph, interface replica, instruction text, or Apple hardware. The active package matches its preserved approved source except for a trailing newline, and the app intentionally exposes no alternate-icon picker. Icon Composer and the system may generate dark, clear, and tinted appearances that aren't explicitly supplied. The separate iPad kiosk correctly compiles a square, opaque, unmasked 1024-by-1024 RGB fallback; visual inspection confirms its centered Wisconsin mark stays inside the masking area. The fallback's missing embedded color profile is the one shared P2 already recorded under Images. No P0/P1 app-icon blocker remains. Home Screen, Settings, notification, search, share-sheet, small-size, Default, Dark, Clear, and Tinted rendering still require compiled simulator or physical-device inspection.
- **Branding:** Wisconsin Creative's authenticated product experience keeps branding subordinate to operational content: system tab, navigation, list, form, sheet, and control patterns own interaction; the mascot/wordmark is confined to startup, sign-in, and forced-password contexts; and the signed-in app does not repeat a logo as decorative chrome. The accepted installed label “Creative” and App Store name “Wisconsin Creative” are documented product choices, while the separate “Wisconsin Kiosk” identity accurately distinguishes the counter appliance. Wisconsin red is the root tint, Gotham Black/Bold is reserved for scalable identity and headline roles with system fallback, body and caption copy use system faces, status colors remain semantic, and no Apple trademark appears in the product name or artwork. No P0/P1 branding blocker remains. The three P2 refinements are the explicit branded launch frame, eleven system-blue accent references that bypass the intended red token, and the previously recorded first-person/technical copy that weakens the otherwise direct operational voice. Cold launch, sign-in, password setup, authenticated restraint, kiosk identity, appearance changes, font fallback, and system-surface brand consistency remain runtime gates.
- **Immersive experiences:** Apple defines this foundation for visionOS Shared Space and Full Space experiences and explicitly marks it unsupported on iOS and iPadOS. Wisconsin Creative declares only iOS application, test, and extension targets (`ios/project.yml:34-35`, `:100-101`, `:150-151`, `:166-167`, `:181-182`, `:197-198`); the main app targets iPhone and iPad while the kiosk targets iPad (`ios/project.yml:92`, `:143`), and both entry points use a conventional SwiftUI `WindowGroup` (`ios/Wisconsin/App/WisconsinApp.swift:32`, `ios/Wisconsin/KioskOnly/KioskOnlyApp.swift:19`). Native source contains no visionOS target, `ImmersiveSpace`, immersion style, RealityKit, ARKit, 3D-environment, passthrough, or virtual-hand implementation. The QR, sticker-linking, and kiosk camera surfaces instead wrap VisionKit's two-dimensional `DataScannerViewController` (`ios/Wisconsin/Views/Search/QRScannerSheet.swift:366-410`, `ios/Wisconsin/Views/DevTools/LinkStickerWizard.swift:260-290`, `ios/Wisconsin/Kiosk/KioskBarcodeCameraView.swift:217-248`) with explicit presentation and recovery paths. No current mobile or counter workflow calls for a visionOS Full Space, so omitting immersive behavior is correct and no P0/P1/P2 finding applies.
- **Inclusion:** the product is designed around concrete student, counter, staff, and admin goals instead of a generic assumed person. Registration asks only for a full name and account credentials and never requests or infers gender, pronouns, sexuality, race, religion, disability, or family structure (`ios/Wisconsin/Views/NativeAuthViews.swift:29-91`). Profile imagery is optional, user-selected, and editable through the system photo picker, with initials as the fallback (`ios/Wisconsin/Views/Welcome/ProfileCompletionWelcomeComponents.swift:485-542`, `ios/Wisconsin/Views/Components/UserAvatarView.swift:5-49`); generic people use nongendered SF Symbols rather than stereotyped artwork. Apparel fields use familiar product-fit and US shoe-sizing labels for an operational clothing need, include a Unisex top fit and free-form Other sizes, and do not treat those labels as identity (`ios/Wisconsin/Models/ProfileCompletionModels.swift:184-192`, `ios/Wisconsin/Views/Welcome/ProfileCompletionWelcomeComponents.swift:426-478`). Role-aware Welcome steps explain one bounded task at a time, support reminder, skip, later re-entry, keyboard flow, error recovery, and accessibility-size layouts. The native product currently declares English only (`ios/Wisconsin.xcodeproj/project.pbxproj:922-928`), while most layout, dates, symbols, and controls remain localization-safe. Nine P1 blockers are the same accessibility repair items already counted by the Accessibility pass, not new inclusion defects. Eight P2 refinements are likewise shared: its six accessibility refinements, the person-/task-centered copy item, and Schedule's future RTL-localization item. No offensive, stereotyped, gender-assumptive, disability-negative, culture-specific humor, or required gender-identity content was found. Representative assistive-technology use, the complete real user/content population, and any future localized build remain runtime or content-governance gates.
- **Privacy:** protected-resource use is narrow: the targets request camera access only after a person enters a scan or camera workflow, notifications reach the system alert only after a deliberate action, profile photos use the system `PhotosPicker`, and no native source requests location, microphone, contacts, health, advertising identifier, or tracking access. Separate main-app and kiosk privacy manifests are bundled in their resource phases, lint successfully, declare tracking false with no tracking domains, and list linked operational data for app functionality. Network traffic keeps arbitrary loads disabled; the signed session remains an HTTP cookie, the kiosk's recoverable session token is device-bound in Keychain, passwords stay in secure fields and request bodies, and passkeys use Apple's platform credential provider with required user verification. Account & Security exposes a prominent self-service deletion command, but the route only deactivates the user and retains the account's personal and authentication data. Inactive scenes receive a window-level privacy shield; widgets publish the signed-in person's non-sensitive operational work and clear/reload at session boundaries; Spotlight likewise indexes only that person's bookings and replaces or deletes its bounded domain. Thumbnail caches, cookies, push credentials, widget data, and Spotlight content have explicit sign-out cleanup. The deactivation-only deletion contract is the remaining P1 blocker; notification delivery truth and the MetricKit file-timestamp declaration are now source-verified. Purpose/pre-alert copy and the automatically timed notification soft prompt are P2. On-device permission alerts, denial/recovery, background screenshots, account switching, archive-generated privacy report, and the live App Store privacy details remain unverified.
- **Charting data:** the native analytics scope stays operational and bounded: Reports uses a common line/area trend for daily checkout activity, a status donut paired with a complete labeled count list, and a horizontal bar ranking for custody days; Scoreboard uses two compact proportion displays only where they directly explain a season record or ranked breakdown. Titles, axes, period context, metric summaries, explanatory footer text, numeric legends, and adjacent record labels communicate purpose and values without relying on color. Swift Charts supplies default per-mark accessibility and Audio Graphs; the custom Scoreboard meters are either replaced by one complete spoken summary or hidden beneath a row label that states the record, game count, and win rate. The one P2 is the checkout trend's duplicated selected-day marks. No P0/P1 charting blocker remains. Actual Audio Graph summaries, VoiceOver mark navigation, touch selection, Dynamic Type, appearance, and representative sparse/dense data remain runtime gates.
- **Collaboration and sharing:** Event Detail is the native app's only outbound content-sharing surface. It uses SwiftUI `ShareLink`, so the system owns service discovery, activity-sheet presentation, cancellation, and iPad adaptation; its adjacent Copy command uses the same plain-text representation. The payload is intentionally limited to display title, localized date/time or all-day state, and venue, making it useful in Messages without leaking crew identities, internal notes, working-copy state, or other account data. The product does not implement a shareable document, participant invitation, CloudKit share, Shared with You item, or synchronized SharePlay activity, so collaboration permission summaries, participant controls, and a Collaboration button would misrepresent the current model. Staff Schedule edits instead remain a server-validated private versioned working copy: pending state, change summary, affected-worker count, timed release, conflict rejection, and deliberate Revert are visible to authorized editors, while workers and collaborators continue seeing the last released schedule. The existing P2 is discoverability: Share Event is in a visible toolbar overflow only when unrelated edit conditions make that menu appear, leaving long press as the sole route for many viewers. No P0/P1 collaboration or sharing blocker remains. Actual activity-sheet content, target apps, VoiceOver discovery, iPad popover anchoring, copy confirmation, and cross-account payload privacy remain runtime gates.
- **Drag and drop:** native source defines no custom draggable content, drop destination, drag/drop delegate, item-provider exchange, or reordering workflow. That omission matches the current task model: equipment selection, staffing, reservations, trades, and custody changes are validated commands with visible tap/menu alternatives rather than freeform object movement between containers; profile imagery enters through the system `PhotosPicker`. The three explicit drag gestures manipulate their visible object directly—dismissing a battery suggestion, paging the Schedule month, and positioning a profile crop—and each has a visible button, calendar controls, or sliders as an alternative. Standard SwiftUI fields retain platform editing behavior, but the custom kiosk `UITextField` bridge defeats UIKit's built-in text drag/drop for replacements of six or more characters; the shared P1 above blocks this pass. No additional drag/drop feature is required by current product contracts. Actual selected-text movement, pointer and touch drops, Full Keyboard Access/VoiceOver drag actions, invalid-drop feedback, and the mounted-kiosk input path remain runtime gates.
- **Entering data:** the native product generally minimizes typing and uses current context well. Invited account setup preloads and locks the approved email; Welcome hydrates existing profile values and asks role-specific fields only; reservations default requester, pickup location, date window, event context, and saved drafts; Schedule authoring derives event windows and configured call times; profile images use `PhotosPicker`; kiosk work begins from identity and equipment scans. Passwords use secure fields and are never prepopulated. Bounded alternatives use date pickers, segmented controls, menus, toggles, steppers, and system photo selection; phone, email, Wiscard, quantity, date-window, staffing, and availability values normalize or validate beside the task, and primary actions disable during invalid or in-flight states. Draft autosave, immutable reservation replay, newest-request ownership, server validation, and recoverable errors protect entered work through interruption and uncertain submission. Three shared P1 repairs block the pattern: invited registration focus enters its disabled email field, the two quarter-hour selectors put 96 values into one menu, and kiosk title fields reject ordinary Paste/drop replacements as scanner bursts. The account-setup validation-discoverability issue above is P2. No additional data-entry blocker was found. Real autofill, password-manager, hardware-keyboard, dictation, Paste/drop, scanner, rotation, interruption, and server-rejection behavior remain runtime gates.
- **Feedback:** the native product generally matches feedback prominence to consequence. Routine saves, follow changes, favorite failures, and similar outcomes use lightweight transient messages; persistent inline states explain loading, empty, unavailable, and recoverable failures; destructive or consequential actions use alerts and confirmations; and kiosk scan workflows pair visible success, warning, and error messages with differentiated haptics and explicit VoiceOver announcements. Kiosk session expiry, activation, checkout, pickup, return, authentication, notification, and badge flows also announce important state changes instead of relying on color or animation alone. Two shared P1 repairs block the pattern: the reusable toast disappears without announcing its message to VoiceOver, and automatic blast/scanner/operator feedback moves vertically without respecting Reduce Motion. The unexpected reservation-creation failure's choice-style dialog and the three unlabeled full-screen initial loaders are the two shared P2 refinements. No additional feedback repair item was created. VoiceOver announcement timing and interruption, Reduce Motion, haptic distinction, slow/failing requests, repeated scans, and representative success/error recovery remain runtime gates.
- **File management:** Wisconsin Creative is an operational client for server-owned reservations, custody, inventory, schedules, reports, guides, and settings rather than a document-based app whose primary content belongs in Files. Neither native target declares document types, opening-in-place, file sharing, an iCloud document container, or a document-browser role, and native source defines no file importer/exporter, `FileDocument`, `DocumentGroup`, `UIDocument`, document picker, security-scoped URL, or Quick Look workflow. That omission matches the accepted surface boundary: broad imports and CSV/report exports remain web control-room work instead of mobile field work. The one user-selected media boundary uses the system `PhotosPicker`, reads only the chosen image, downsamples it, and exposes a specific choose-another recovery message; the one outbound event-sharing boundary uses `ShareLink` with a bounded text representation rather than manufacturing a file. MetricKit JSON is implementation-owned diagnostic data, written atomically into protected Application Support, excluded from backup, capped through pruning, and never presented as a user document. No current workflow creates a file people reasonably expect to rename, move, duplicate, browse, or reopen elsewhere, so adding Files UI would misrepresent the product model. No P0/P1/P2 finding applies. System Photos picker cancellation/provider behavior, share-sheet destinations, protected-file availability after restart, pruning under storage pressure, and any future native import/export remain runtime or future-feature gates.
- **Going full screen:** full-screen presentation is bounded to experiences that benefit from focus: one-shot global search scanning, continuous reservation equipment scanning, and an optional item-photo viewer. Both scanner hosts use the same system `fullScreenCover`; `QRScannerSheet` keeps essential Close, torch, typed-code, progress, warning, retry-adjacent, and continuous-scan controls visible in safe-area insets, provides camera-denial and VoiceOver alternatives, stops the torch when inactive or dismissed, and leaves ordinary Home Screen and Dock gestures undeferred. One-shot lookup exits only after completing its explicit scan task, while reservation scanning stays open until the person closes it. The photo viewer keeps content prominent, supports direct pinch and optional double-tap zoom, and offers backdrop dismissal plus a labeled Close button, but that explicit exit is only 38 points; the shared interaction-target repair therefore blocks this pass. Its missing image/loading/failure context and Retry plus unguarded double-tap scaling are two shared P2 refinements. The dedicated counter kiosk appropriately uses a distraction-free full-screen root, keeps every workflow control in-app, pauses camera/torch work with scene changes, retains normal system-exit gestures, and has compact-layout source defenses; its restored landscape-only `UIRequiresFullScreen` contract remains the third P2 because Xcode 26 warns that enforcement will disappear in a future release. No additional full-screen finding was created. Simulator/device proof remains open for safe-area placement, Home indicator and Dock revelation, app switching/resume, rotation and resized kiosk scenes, VoiceOver modal context, photo load failure, and physical camera interruption.
- **Launching:** the main app does substantial work to reach useful content quickly without weakening session authority. A safe cached identity snapshot can draw the correct shell and fresh-data skeleton immediately while `/me` validates in the background; confirmed 401 clears the snapshot and routes to Login, while a network failure preserves an optimistic shell or shows a recoverable offline Login state. Strict forced-password launches never use the optimistic bypass. When a blocking restore is necessary, `LaunchView` avoids an artificial minimum duration, reveals labeled progress only after 650 milliseconds, escalates the message after four seconds, announces both changes to VoiceOver, and respects Reduce Motion. Root routing selects forced password, role-aware Welcome, read-only preview, app shell, or Login from authoritative state; launch/session/Home phases carry signposts and duration logs. The kiosk similarly restores its device-bound credential behind a labeled resume splash instead of flashing activation, then falls back to activation if validation fails. The shared P1 is the four Home Screen quick actions: the scene-based app implements only the deprecated `UIApplicationDelegate.application(_:performActionFor:completionHandler:)` route and no scene connection or warm-action handler, so those advertised launch paths are unsupported; the focused test passes because it requires the obsolete callback rather than detecting it. Two P2 refinements remain shared: process recreation restores account and recoverable reservation work but loses the selected tab and pushed navigation position, and the static system launch screen uses a branded logo/wordmark image instead of downplaying launch with a first-screen continuity frame. The latter can also flash directly into a light optimistic shell even though its branded dark frame matches the blocking restore and Login surfaces. No additional launching finding was created. Cold/warm launch timing, every orientation and appearance, background eviction, offline/slow/401 restoration, quick actions, Spotlight/widgets/deep links, VoiceOver announcements, and installed kiosk resume remain runtime gates.
- **Live-viewing apps:** this pattern does not apply to Wisconsin Creative's current native product scope. The iOS source has no audiovisual player, live stream, channel browser, electronic program guide, playback transport, picture-in-picture controller, Now Playing session, remote-command integration, or DVR behavior. Its `AVFoundation` imports serve barcode/QR camera capture and torch control rather than content viewing. The similarly named checkout-return Live Activity is an ActivityKit glance surface: it presents booking identity, a locally advancing return countdown, urgency/returned state, accessibility copy, and a booking deep link across the Lock Screen, Dynamic Island, CarPlay, and Apple Watch; it neither plays nor navigates live media. Omitting live-viewing chrome is therefore the HIG-aligned choice, with no finding. Any future live audiovisual product surface must reopen this pass for immediate playback, live-versus-on-demand identity, channel browsing, program-guide, audio-session, picture-in-picture, and recording behavior.
- **Loading:** the native product usually follows Apple's loading model well. Home, Items, Bookings, Users, Schedule, details, and Scoreboard show immediate shape-matched skeletons rather than blank visual canvases; Guides and Licenses add explicit loading labels; kiosk restore, roster, assignment, and gear waits identify the pending task; and launch delays its progress treatment until the wait becomes perceptible. Existing content generally remains usable while refreshes run, independent data sources load concurrently where useful, first-use instrumentation measures the Home path, list pagination has bounded loading rows plus inline Retry, and initial, empty, stale, partial, offline, and terminal error states stay distinct. The shared P1 is the inaccessible first-load skeleton contract: decorative shapes are hidden correctly, but several primary screens provide no equivalent VoiceOver status. Four existing P2 refinements also apply: Notifications, Reports, and Overdue Report use task-agnostic full-screen spinners; two discovery thumbnails render clear content during load and after failure instead of preserving their semantic placeholders; the full-screen photo viewer has no named loading, failure, or retry state; and several pull-to-refresh handlers can dismiss without starting a fresh request when older work is active. No large background asset pack or game-level download exists, so Background Assets and determinate byte progress are not required. Slow-network timing, VoiceOver announcements, stale-content interaction, pagination exhaustion/retry, image failure, pull-to-refresh overlap, cancellation, and scene interruption remain runtime gates.
- **Managing accounts:** identity is legitimately required at entry because every useful Wisconsin Creative workflow contains private account, schedule, reservation, custody, profile, or role-scoped data; there is no honest public-browsing mode to delay sign-in into. Email discovery reveals only onboarding versus password, invited registration locks the approved address, password fields identify their username for AutoFill, recovery stays native, forced-password setup blocks the authenticated shell, and passkeys use Apple's platform provider with discoverable login, current-password-protected enrollment/removal, storage labels, and cancellation-safe feedback. Authentication methods are named, no app-specific biometric toggle exists, profile/security and active-session management are easy to reach, password changes can revoke other sessions, sign-out is confirmed, and client account caches, widgets, Spotlight, push credentials, and account-scoped state are cleared across session boundaries. Two P1 blockers remain: registration keyboard progression targets its locked email field, and the prominent permanent Delete Account flow only deactivates the user while retaining account and authentication data. Six P2 refinements apply: hidden registration requirements, the staged Sign out other devices toggle, a potentially overlong passkey-removal action-sheet title, sign-in copy that explains invitation mechanics but not account value, capability-insensitive Face ID/Touch ID wording, and password-only authentication without a second factor or passkey-first enrollment. The deletion path also needs an explicit visual/spoken completion state and truthful handling when downstream companion revocation fails after the deactivation transaction commits; these are part of the same deletion blocker rather than separate counts. Real invitation claim, password-manager save/update, passkey enrollment/login/removal, lost-device recovery, multi-session revocation, account deletion/retention, VoiceOver, interruption, offline, and installed-device proof remain open.
- **Managing notifications:** the system prompt appears only after a clear, cancellable value explanation and a deliberate Enable tap; authorization denial routes to iOS Settings, previously authorized accounts re-register without asking again, and APNs registration truth is shown separately with Retry and a device-specific test. Native Settings offers a global push switch plus seven operational categories while preserving an always-available in-app inbox. Server delivery honors the global channel, pause, category, active-user, platform, and revoked-token boundaries; invalid tokens are retired. Categories and safe long-press actions are registered at launch, destructive mutations are not exposed from the Lock Screen, a plain tap and View share bounded account-safe routing, and sign-out clears tokens, delivered and pending alerts, and counts. The inbox distinguishes loading, empty, error, pagination, unread, optimistic read/revert, and target routing states. The app intentionally uses no Home Screen app-icon badge, avoiding a second stale count, and ActivityKit checkout-return updates remain a separate opt-in glance surface. One P1 blocks the pass: native Settings ignores a durable account pause and can claim delivery is enabled while normal alerts are suppressed. Four P2 refinements apply: automatic post-login soft-prompt timing, one default Active interruption level for every ordinary alert, unconditional foreground banner/sound presentation, and local snoozes that survive a later in-app pause or push opt-out. Real APNs delivery, Focus and Scheduled Summary behavior, notification-preview privacy, action execution, deep-link authorization, foreground suppression, pause/opt-out reconciliation, device revocation, VoiceOver, and physical-device proof remain open.
- **Modality:** native modality is generally reserved for a clear benefit: account recovery, focused creation/editing, filter or selection tasks, notifications and Trade Board browsing, system confirmation, camera capture, and optional photo inspection. Full-screen presentation is limited to camera and photo work that benefits from immersion. Modal titles usually identify the task; short selection/filter sheets apply immediately or end with one result action; alerts and confirmation dialogs are reserved for consequential choices or failures; destructive actions include an escape; and reservation, item, booking, availability, schedule, trade, account, and photo-editing tasks disable dismissal during critical work and confirm before discarding user input. Notifications correctly dismisses before routing to a booking, item, user, event, or subsequent Trade Board sheet. Two shared P1s block the pass: several primary flows stack or branch modal experiences—including the app-like Profile sheet—and passive browsing sheets use a lone Done dismissal with inconsistent cancellation/confirmation placement. No additional P2 applies. Simulator and iPad proof remain open for detents, drag dismissal, interactive-dismiss confirmation, keyboard and VoiceOver focus, alert exclusivity, nested-presentation transitions, rotation, interruption, and state restoration.
- **Offering help:** the product uses help at three appropriate depths. Simple feature discovery uses seven short, direct TipKit popovers attached to reservation, Schedule, availability, scanning, minimizing, and resuming controls; action completion invalidates the corresponding tip, later or state-dependent features use event rules, and every tip is limited to one display. Inline empty, unavailable, permission, validation, and recovery states describe the immediate task and normally provide the relevant Retry, Settings, Clear filters, camera, or next-step action instead of explaining standard controls. The kiosk scanner keeps its normal state guidance concise and exposes a dedicated Scanner Health sheet only on request, with current status, three targeted checks, and a camera fallback. Complex reference and troubleshooting needs live in the searchable native Guides library: role/area-aware ranking, typed focus, summaries, full-text excerpt search, freshness metadata, safe Markdown callouts, and retry paths keep long-form help separate from task UI while remaining reachable through Browse or the regular-width sidebar. No P0/P1 blocker remains. Two P2 refinements apply: configure a shared TipKit cadence, and make the first Schedule tip match the Trade Board control and recipient capabilities. Actual TipKit eligibility/cadence, dismissal and VoiceOver focus, empty/error action usefulness, scanner troubleshooting on mounted hardware, guide relevance/search, Dynamic Type, and offline help availability remain runtime gates.
- **Onboarding:** the app avoids a generic first-run carousel and separates launch, identity, prerequisite account security, profile setup, contextual discovery, and long-form help. Invited registration asks only for name, approved email, and password; direct-created accounts complete the required password change before entering the shell. The role-aware Welcome flow begins at the first incomplete server-owned step, explains one task at a time, saves each completed step, resumes after interruption, supports Back, and lets people defer the entire prompt until tomorrow. Apparel and photo are explicitly optional, collaborators see only the skippable photo step, load failure offers Retry or Continue for now, and incomplete setup remains reachable later from Profile. Feature learning then occurs through control-attached TipKit messages and the searchable Guides library instead of requiring memorization before use. No P0/P1 blocker remains. Three shared P2 refinements apply: move notification education from the automatic 600-millisecond post-login presentation to a relevant action, configure a shared TipKit cadence, and make the first Schedule tip match both its Trade Board anchor and recipient capabilities. No additional onboarding finding was created. First registration, password-manager behavior, forced-password completion, deferral and next-day recurrence, interrupted-step recovery, TipKit persistence/audience/cadence, permission handoff, Dynamic Type, VoiceOver, offline behavior, and representative role data remain runtime gates.
- **Playing audio:** neither native target offers media playback, spoken content, audio recording, calling, or custom interface sounds, so playback controls, system volume and route pickers, headphone disconnection, Now Playing/remote commands, audio-session categories, background playback, and interruption/resume policy do not apply to the current product. The project declares no microphone purpose string or audio background mode and contains no bundled audio or video media. All four AVFoundation imports are confined to barcode/QR camera authorization, torch access, or VisionKit scanning and request only `.video`; they never add an audio capture input or configure `AVAudioSession`. Short interaction feedback uses system haptic generators or SwiftUI sensory feedback rather than unexpected custom sound. Ordinary push and locally snoozed notification alerts request only the system default notification sound, leaving silent mode, Focus, output, and volume behavior to iOS; every inspected alert also carries visible title/body or in-app state rather than depending on sound alone. No P0/P1/P2 finding applies. Physical silent-mode, notification-sound, connected-output, and scanner-haptic behavior remain runtime gates, and this pass must be revisited if the app adds audio/video media, recording, spoken guidance, or custom sounds.
- **Playing haptics:** success, warning, and error outcomes consistently use the matching system notification patterns, while custom list choices and day/filter changes generally use the standard selection pattern. Haptics remain short, discrete, and supplemental: mutation results have persistent or transient text, scan workflows show banners and announce important outcomes, selection state remains visible, and the product has no Core Haptics engine, custom waveform, continuous vibration, sound-synchronized effect, controller haptics, or Apple Pencil interaction. The two native targets deliberately expose equivalent generator semantics even though their source membership is split. No P0/P1 blocker remains. Three P2 refinements apply: provide an app-level mute preference, retire the generic light-impact `tap` abstraction, and remove duplicate or async-delayed selection pulses from standard controls. Installed-device proof remains open for intensity and timing, global/app mute behavior, rapid repeated scans and edits, camera stability during scan feedback, VoiceOver operation, and actual support differences between iPhone and the mounted iPad kiosk.
- **Playing video:** neither native target contains AVKit, `AVPlayer`, `VideoPlayer`, `AVPlayerViewController`, Picture in Picture, Now Playing, remote-command, subtitle/caption, playback-position, or media-resume code, and no video file or HLS playlist is bundled. The four AVFoundation consumers are barcode/QR scanner surfaces that request only camera video, never record or retain a movie, and provide visible exit, permission, manual-entry, failure, and task-feedback paths. Guides do recognize fenced YouTube, Vimeo, and other HTTP(S) video URLs, but the native renderer explicitly avoids an inline provider player: it presents a labeled external-link card, names the provider and host, says “Opens in browser” to accessibility, and hands playback to the system destination (`ios/Wisconsin/Views/GuideMarkdown.swift:158-177`, `:498-515`; `ios/Wisconsin/Views/GuidesView.swift:829-868`; `docs/GUIDE_MARKDOWN.md:56-71`). System-player interaction, aspect ratio, PiP, captions, keyboard playback control, loading/resume, secondary-audio, full-screen exit, and TV-app integration therefore do not apply to the current native product. No P0/P1/P2 finding applies. External-link handoff, browser/app return, camera interruption, orientation, VoiceOver, and representative provider accessibility remain runtime gates; revisit the complete pass before adding inline or bundled playback, recording, preview video, or an app-owned media queue.
- **Printing:** neither native target creates a printable document or uses `UIPrintInteractionController`, `UIPrintInfo`, a print formatter/page renderer, printer discovery, PDFKit, `FileDocument`, a document exporter, or an activity controller carrying print-ready data. The only native `ShareLink` sends bounded event text, not a file or formatted page. Omitting Print is consistent with the accepted surface contract: inventory and battery labels, report/CSV output, license export, schedule export, and broad data export are staff/admin control-room workflows owned by the web app, while iOS and the kiosk focus on field actions, scanning, reservation work, custody, and concise operational reads (`docs/AREA_MOBILE.md:58`, `:366`, `:489`; `tasks/audit-hig-ios.md:241`). The web label queue owns content selection, print styling, browser Print, unprinted/printed state, retry, and accessibility; numbered-unit label export separately requires an explicit post-export confirmation before marking physical labels printed. Because there is no printable native content, hiding Print everywhere is more truthful than exposing a disabled or incomplete command, and system printer/options UI, page range, copies, duplex, preview, and cancellation don't yet apply. No P0/P1/P2 finding applies. Revisit the pass if a native view gains PDF/label/report output or if field operators need AirPrint; at that point verify system action-sheet placement, print availability, page rendering, options, cancellation, VoiceOver, iPad presentation, and physical-printer output.
- **Ratings and reviews:** neither native target imports StoreKit, calls `RequestReviewAction` or `SKStoreReviewController`, presents a custom rating/sentiment prompt, or links directly to an App Store review surface. That absence is appropriate for the current invited operational audience: Apple does not require an in-app prompt, and injecting one into reservation, Schedule, scanning, custody, or other time-sensitive work would add interruption without a product need. Settings instead provides a stable Contact Support mail link (`ios/Wisconsin/Views/SettingsView.swift:21`, `:112-119`), so people can report a problem without being screened by sentiment or steered away from a public rating. No first-launch/onboarding request, task-completion ambush, repeated cadence, incentive, review manipulation, or custom star UI was found, so no P0/P1/P2 finding applies. If review prompting is introduced later, use the system request action only after demonstrated engagement and at a natural stopping point, never on first launch or during onboarding, require meaningful new engagement between attempts, keep support available independently, and verify the system's display discretion rather than treating a request call as a guaranteed prompt.
- **Requesting permission:** Apple's former standalone pattern is retired; its current canonical guidance is the Requesting permission section under Privacy, so this pass records that live source without creating a duplicate foundation finding.
